370 — Arbitrary-Precision Arithmetic (Bignum) from Scratch

Project Number: 370

Project Name: Arbitrary-Precision Arithmetic (Bignum) from Scratch

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 What Is Built

A from-scratch arbitrary-precision integer arithmetic library in Rust, in the lineage of GMP, Java BigInteger, and Python int. The library stores unsigned magnitudes as little-endian Vec<u64> limb vectors and layers signed integers and exact rationals on top. Every algorithm tier — schoolbook, Karatsuba, Toom-Cook-3, NTT-based, Knuth Algorithm D, Newton-reciprocal division, Barrett/Montgomery reduction, binary/Lehmer GCD, divide-and-conquer base conversion — is implemented from first principles, tested differentially against reference oracles, and dispatched by operand size.

1.2 System Boundaries

Boundary In scope Out of scope
Arithmetic Add/sub/mul/div/rem/mod, GCD, pow, modpow, base conversion, rationals Floating-point (IEEE-754 soft-float is project #401)
Storage In-process Vec<Limb> heap allocation SIMD asm optimization in Phase 0 (optional later)
Cryptography Constant-time modpow path (P9) Full TLS/RSA/EC key management
I/O Decimal/hex/arbitrary-base string parse+format Binary serialization (serde feature, post-Phase 14)
Platform stable x86_64 and aarch64 Linux/macOS; any target compiling u64/u128 WASM (size budget differs — deferred)

1.3 Explicit Non-Goals

  • No floating-point approximation; see project #401 for IEEE-754 soft-float.
  • No prime generation, factoring, or elliptic-curve primitives (consumers: projects #87–91).
  • No no_std in Phase 0; alloc-only is PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register").
  • No SIMD/AVX-512 assembly kernels in Phase 0; optional in Phase 14 after correctness is proven.
  • No serde derive in Phase 0; deferred to post-Phase 14.
  • No parallelism (multi-threaded multiply); the library is single-threaded.

1.4 Intended Deployment Model

Role Usage pattern
Library consumer Link bn-int / bn-rational as a Cargo dependency in cryptographic, compiler, or mathematical software
CLI user bn-cli calc for interactive arithmetic; bn-cli bench for threshold tuning
Downstream projects RSA (#88), zk-SNARK (#92), EVM big-int (#183), modular arithmetic in any portfolio project
Fuzz target bn-cli subcommands as cargo-fuzz harnesses

1.5 Phase 0 Deliverable — Locked Crates

A workspace Cargo.toml declares the following members; all must compile with zero warnings on stable Rust:

  1. bn-core — Limb, DoubleLimb, SignedDoubleLimb, Sign, ParseError, DivByZero, MulAlgo; limb constants (LIMB_BITS, LIMB_MAX); no I/O, no heap allocation beyond types.
  2. bn-uint — BigUint { limbs: Vec<Limb> }; normalization, comparison, from/to u64/u128, shifts, bit utilities.
  3. bn-add — in-place add/sub with carry/borrow; add_mul_1 helper; result length invariants.
  4. bn-mul — schoolbook, squaring, Karatsuba, Toom-Cook-3; MulAlgo dispatch by limb count; threshold constants.
  5. bn-fftmul — NTT-based multiply; prime set, CRT recombination; NttPlan.
  6. bn-div — Knuth Algorithm D; Newton-reciprocal divrem; DivRem; single-limb fast path.
  7. bn-mod — Barrett (Barrett context); Montgomery (Montgomery context, REDC); windowed modpow.
  8. bn-gcd — binary (Stein) GCD; Lehmer GCD; extended GCD; modular inverse.
  9. bn-int — BigInt { sign: Sign, mag: BigUint }; signed arithmetic, comparison, two's-complement bitwise ops.
  10. bn-convert — base parse/format; schoolbook and divide-and-conquer paths; roundtrip invariant.
  11. bn-rational — BigRational { sign, num, den }; reduced arithmetic; canonical sign.
  12. bn-cli — calc, bench, convert, factorial subcommands wiring the full stack.

1.6 What Later Phases Unlock

Phase Capability unlocked
Phase 2 Reliable add/sub: all higher algorithms depend on it
Phase 4 Schoolbook multiply oracle for differential testing in Phases 5–6
Phase 6 NTT multiply; exact large products without floating-point rounding
Phase 7–8 Division; enables GCD, base conversion, modular inverse
Phase 10–11 Constant-time modpow; enables RSA (#88) and zk-SNARK (#92) consumers
Phase 12 Human-readable I/O; enables bn-cli usability
Phase 13 BigRational; enables symbolic computation consumers
Phase 14 Hardening, fuzz, conformance, packing; library is production-ready

1.7 Deferred Features

  • no_std / alloc-only target: PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register")
  • SIMD/AVX-512 limb kernels: PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register")
  • serde serialization: PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register")
  • Parallel multiply (Rayon): PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register")
  • Decimal float overlay: PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register")

1.8 Timeline

Scope Estimate
Phase 0 (workspace scaffold + bn-core + bn-uint) 1–2 weeks
Phases 1–8 (full unsigned arithmetic) 8–14 weeks total
Phases 9–13 (signed, modular, rationals, I/O) 4–8 weeks
Phase 14 (hardening, fuzz, conformance, benchmarks) 2–4 weeks
Full library 14–28 weeks

1.9 Language Rationale — Rust (Locked)

Bignum is limb-level arithmetic: every operation is a loop over machine words propagating carries/borrows, where capturing the carry out of a u64 add demands a u128 (or add-with-carry) and a single mishandled carry corrupts every higher digit. Rust gives word-level control (u64 limbs, u128 double-limb intermediates, wrapping/carrying ops, optional inline asm for adc/mulx), zero-cost slices over the limb buffer, and exhaustive sign enums — all without a GC pause on the multiply/divide hot path. The whole library (magnitude, signed, modular, FFT/NTT multiply, division, rationals) is Rust; hardware add-with-carry intrinsics and GMP-style assembly kernels are an optional later optimization, not Phase 0.

1.10 Prerequisites

Domain Required prior knowledge
CPU word arithmetic Two's complement, carry/borrow, unsigned overflow, u64×u64→u128 widening
Rust Ownership, Vec<T> internals, trait objects, std::ops operator overloading, #[inline]
Number theory Modular arithmetic, Euclidean algorithm, Fermat's little theorem, Chinese remainder theorem
Algorithm complexity O(n²) schoolbook vs O(n^1.585) Karatsuba vs O(n log n log log n) NTT asymptote
Numerical analysis Newton–Raphson iteration convergence, fixed-point reciprocal approximation

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge Taught

Topic Lesson delivered
Limb-level carry propagation The exact mechanics of multi-precision add/sub; why a single missed carry is catastrophic
Multiplication algorithm tiers Schoolbook → Karatsuba → Toom-Cook-3 → NTT: each threshold is an empirical engineering decision, not a theoretical one
Number-theoretic transforms Integer-exact large multiplication without floating-point; NTT primes, roots of unity, CRT recombination
Knuth Algorithm D The canonical normalized long-division algorithm; quotient-digit estimation, correction, add-back
Newton-reciprocal division Converting division to multiplication via iterative convergence; why it beats long division above ~100 limbs
Barrett and Montgomery reduction Precomputed reciprocals for repeated modular reduction; REDC without trial division
Constant-time modpow Why timing side-channels in modular exponentiation break RSA; how to eliminate data-dependent branches
Lehmer GCD Matrix-based GCD that reduces O(n) single-precision steps to O(1) limb-level work per Euclidean step
Divide-and-conquer base conversion Why schoolbook repeated-divmod is O(n²) and how recursive splitting achieves O(M(n) log n)
Sign-magnitude discipline Why separating sign from magnitude simplifies every kernel and where the discipline breaks (two's-complement bitwise ops)

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

  • GNU MP (GMP) is the performance reference: every serious bignum library either wraps it or benchmarks against it.
  • Python's int, Java's BigInteger, and Haskell's Integer all implement the same algorithm tier progression; understanding one is understanding all.
  • RSA, Diffie-Hellman, elliptic-curve cryptography, zk-SNARKs, and homomorphic encryption all reduce to repeated modular bignum operations; this library is the substrate.
  • Compilers (constant folding of large integers), interpreters (arbitrary-precision number literals), and CAS (computer algebra systems) all depend on bignum correctness for semantic integrity.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

  • Correctness-first culture: differential testing against schoolbook/Knuth establishes an unbreakable oracle discipline.
  • Asymptotic awareness: choosing algorithm tiers and measuring crossover thresholds empirically, not theoretically.
  • Allocation discipline: in-place limb buffer reuse; identifying per-inner-loop allocs and eliminating them.
  • Side-channel awareness: data-independent execution paths; understanding where if secret_bit leaks timing.
  • Property-based testing: generators for arbitrary limb counts, signed values, rational pairs; invariant checking after every operation.
  • Fuzz-driven hardening: cargo-fuzz harnesses on parse, divmod, modpow; crash triage and minimization.

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept Where exercised
Zero-copy slices Limbs<'a> / LimbsMut<'a> kernel signatures; no unnecessary cloning
Buffer reuse DivRem scratch space; Newton iteration temps; NTT butterfly buffer
Typed errors ParseError, DivByZero — never panics on invalid input
Operator overloading std::ops::{Add, Sub, Mul, Div, Rem, Neg, BitAnd, BitOr, BitXor} for ergonomic API
Benchmark-driven tuning criterion benches at 1/4/16/64/256/1024 limb sizes locate threshold crossovers
Conformance testing Random operands cross-checked against num-bigint / Python int / GMP via a thin FFI shim

2.5 Cross-Links to Portfolio Projects

Project Relationship
#87 AES / #88 SHA Consumers of modular reduction and byte-level big-endian conversion
#89 RSA Primary consumer: modpow, modular inverse, key generation from large primes
#90 Curve25519 Field arithmetic over a prime field; Montgomery ladder needs constant-time modmul
#91 Argon2 Memory-hard KDF; uses big-integer hash mixing
#92 zk-SNARK Polynomial arithmetic over large prime fields; heavily exercises NTT multiply and Montgomery
#182–184 Blockchain/EVM/Merkle-Patricia EVM MULMOD/ADDMOD/EXP opcodes; 256-bit bignum is the substrate
#401 IEEE-754 soft-float The real-number sibling; shares limb-level carry and rounding concerns
Compiler projects Constant-folding of arbitrary-precision integer literals; semantic correctness depends on this library

2.6 Calibration Value

Building this library from scratch demonstrates mastery at the intersection of number theory, systems programming, and performance engineering — a rare combination. An engineer who has implemented all 14 phases can read GMP source, diagnose bignum bugs in production cryptographic libraries, and make informed algorithm-selection decisions that are invisible to engineers who only use bignum as a black box. The project is a permanent portfolio anchor for cryptographic systems, compiler, and high-performance numeric computing roles.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

After building this, you can demonstrably:

  • Implement carry-correct limb arithmetic with u128 double-limb capture and never drop a carry — built in Phase 2 (add/sub) and Phase 4 (schoolbook multiply).
  • Select a multiplication algorithm by operand size and derive the crossover points analytically — built in Phase 5 (Karatsuba/Toom-Cook) and Phase 6 (NTT).
  • Make an integer transform exact via multi-prime NTT + CRT, and prove no rounding can occur — built in Phase 6 (NTT multiplication).
  • Turn division into multiplication with Newton-reciprocal iteration and know when Knuth D is still faster — built in Phase 7 (Knuth) and Phase 8 (Newton divrem).
  • Build the modular hot loop of cryptography with cached Barrett/Montgomery contexts and sliding-window exponentiation — built in Phase 10 (modular arithmetic) and Phase 11 (modpow/GCD/inverse).
  • Reason about constant-time as a control-flow property, not a math property, and gate it behind an opt-in path — built in Phase 11 (ct-modpow).
  • Validate a fast-path tower against a trusted oracle via differential testing — built in Phase 14 (conformance vs num-bigint / Python int).

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

                        ARBITRARY-PRECISION ARITHMETIC  (bignum library)
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|   bn-cli (calc / bench / convert / factorial)                                       |
|        |                                                                            |
|        v                                                                            |
|   bn-rational (exact num/den, gcd-reduced)                                          |
|        |                                                                            |
|        v                                                                            |
|   bn-int  (Sign + BigUint; two's-complement bitops, signed compare)                  |
|        |                                                                            |
|        v                                                                            |
|   +-------------------------- bn-uint (BigUint magnitude) -----------------------+   |
|   |  normalization (no trailing zero limbs)  |  comparison  |  shifts            |   |
|   +----+-----------+-----------+-----------+-----------+-----------+-------------+   |
|        |           |           |           |           |           |               |
|        v           v           v           v           v           v               |
|    bn-add      bn-mul      bn-fftmul    bn-div      bn-mod      bn-gcd               |
|   (carry/      (school/    (NTT +       (Knuth D,   (Barrett,   (binary,            |
|    borrow)      Karatsuba/  CRT)         Newton      Montgomery  Lehmer,            |
|                 Toom3)                   reciprocal) REDC)       ext-gcd)            |
|        |           |           |           |           |           |               |
|        +-----------+-----------+-----------+-----------+-----------+               |
|                                |                                                   |
|                                v                                                   |
|   bn-convert (base<->base, parse/format; divide-and-conquer for large)              |
|                                |                                                   |
|                                v                                                   |
|   bn-core (Limb/DoubleLimb/Sign; the shared word + sign vocabulary)                  |
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

bn-core — The zero-dependency foundation. Defines Limb = u64, DoubleLimb = u128, SignedDoubleLimb = i128, Sign { Minus, NoSign, Plus }, MulAlgo, NttPlan, ParseError, DivByZero, Base. No heap allocation, no I/O. All other crates depend on it; it depends on nothing in the workspace. Data flows out only: type definitions and constants are consumed everywhere.

bn-uint — The unsigned magnitude layer. BigUint { limbs: Vec<Limb> } stores the little-endian magnitude. Enforces I1 (no trailing zeros) on every mutation via normalize(). Provides comparison (length-then-high-limb-descending), from/to u64/u128, shift-left/right by bit count, bit-length, count_ones, trailing_zeros, set_bit, test_bit. All unsigned algorithms operate on BigUint or borrowed Limbs<'a> slices. No sign semantics here. Data in: raw Vec<Limb> construction or primitive casts. Data out: normalized BigUint references consumed by all arithmetic crates.

bn-add — Carry/borrow propagation kernels. add_assign(a, b) loops limb-by-limb accumulating carry in a u128; result may grow by one limb. sub_assign(a, b) requires a >= b (unsigned contract); borrows via i128. add_mul_1(a, b, c: Limb) computes a += b * c — the inner kernel consumed by schoolbook multiply and Newton iteration. All functions operate in-place on LimbsMut<'a> to avoid allocation. Data in/out: mutable limb slice pairs.

bn-mul — Multiplication dispatch. mul(a, b) -> BigUint selects MulAlgo from a.limbs.len() and b.limbs.len() against locked threshold constants. Schoolbook is the correctness oracle (Phase 4). Karatsuba splits at the midpoint, recurses on three half-sized products. Toom-Cook-3 evaluates at five points, interpolates with exact integer arithmetic. For operands above the NTT threshold, delegates to bn-fftmul. Squaring has a dedicated path (~2× faster). Data in: two BigUint references. Data out: owned BigUint product.

bn-fftmul — NTT-based multiplication (Phase 6). Constructs an NttPlan (prime set, primitive roots, transform length). Forward-transforms both operands into frequency domain over each NTT prime, pointwise-multiplies, inverse-transforms, then CRT-recombines to recover the exact integer product. No floating-point at any step (I8). Used only above the NTT threshold; the plan is reusable for equal-length operands. Data in: two BigUint references + NttPlan. Data out: exact BigUint product.

bn-div — Division algorithms. divrem_knuth(a, b) -> DivRem normalizes the divisor (shift so top bit set), estimates quotient digits from top two numerator limbs, corrects by multiply-subtract, applies add-back on over-estimate (I5). divrem_newton(a, b) -> DivRem computes floor(base^k / b) by Newton iteration using bn-mul, then derives the quotient by a single large multiply plus a small correction (I6). Selects Knuth for small operands; Newton for large. Single-limb divmod is a separate fast path. Data in: two BigUint. Data out: DivRem { quotient, remainder }.

bn-mod — Modular reduction contexts. Barrett { modulus, mu } precomputes mu = floor(base^{2k} / m) once; barrett_reduce(x, ctx) uses two multiplications and two subtractions (I7). Montgomery { modulus, n_prime, r2 } transforms operands into Montgomery form; redc(t, ctx) performs a carry-free reduction (I7). modpow(base, exp, modulus) uses sliding-window exponentiation over Montgomery form; the constant-time path (P9) uses a fixed window and avoids secret-dependent branches. Data in: BigUint operands + context. Data out: reduced BigUint in [0, m).

bn-gcd — GCD and extended GCD. Binary (Stein) GCD: replace subtractions with shifts using trailing_zeros (Phase 3 dependency). Lehmer GCD: extract the top 2×LIMB_BITS bits, run single-precision Euclidean steps in i64, apply resulting 2×2 matrix to the full limb vectors — reduces limb-level work from O(n) to O(1) per Euclidean step. Extended GCD returns Bezout coefficients as BigInt. Modular inverse via extended GCD; Fermat inverse (base^{m-2} mod m) as an alternative for prime moduli. Data in/out: BigUint pairs; extended GCD returns signed BigInt coefficients.

bn-int — Signed arithmetic. BigInt { sign: Sign, mag: BigUint } delegates all magnitude work to bn-uint and bn-add/bn-mul/bn-div. Sign rules: add/sub dispatch on sign comparison; negation flips sign; multiply/divide signs multiply. Two's-complement bitwise ops (AND, OR, XOR, NOT) operate on conceptually sign-extended infinite representations; implemented via case analysis on sign pairs. Enforces I2 (NoSign iff magnitude is zero). Data flow: wraps BigUint; used by bn-rational and bn-cli.

bn-convert — Base conversion and string I/O. Small-number path: repeated divrem by base^chunk accumulating digits (O(n²) acceptable below threshold). Large-number path: divide-and-conquer — split by base^{2^k}, recurse, concatenate with zero-padding (O(M(n) log n)). Parse validates input characters, errors on invalid via ParseError (I9). Format pads to full digit width within each chunk. Supports Base10, Base16, BaseN(u32). Data in: BigUint + Base. Data out: String or BigUint.

bn-rational — Exact rationals. BigRational { sign: Sign, num: BigUint, den: BigUint } maintains gcd(num, den) = 1, den > 0, sign on numerator (I10). Construction always reduces via bn-gcd. Arithmetic: a/b + c/d = (a*d + b*c) / (b*d) followed by reduction; multiply/divide similarly. Comparison by cross-multiply. Canonical sign: numerator carries the sign, denominator is always positive. Conversion to decimal string via divmod with configurable precision. Data in/out: pairs of BigInt or BigUint; used by bn-cli.

bn-cli — The end-to-end exerciser. Four subcommands: calc (REPL-style expression evaluator over BigInt/BigRational), bench (prints per-size timing for mul/div/modpow, used to tune thresholds), convert (base conversion with arbitrary radix), factorial (large-factorial via divide-and-conquer tree). No library logic lives here; all dispatch is through the public API surface. Acts as the integration test harness and the primary demonstration artifact.

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

Constraint Target Notes
Correctness — schoolbook oracle agreement 100 % match for all inputs ≤ 512 limbs I4; verified by differential property tests
Correctness — Knuth vs Newton agreement 100 % match for all inputs I6; tested across size classes
NTT exactness Zero rounding error for all supported sizes I8; enforced by integer-only transforms
Base-conversion roundtrip Exact for all bases 2–36 and all sizes I9; property test on random values
Memory ceiling — single operand ≤ 8 MB limb buffer (1 M limbs × 8 bytes) Practical limit; library is unbounded by design
Peak scratch allocation — schoolbook mul ≤ 2 × (n + m) limbs Result buffer + optional temp; no per-step allocs
Peak scratch allocation — NTT multiply ≤ 6 × padded transform length (limbs) Three prime transforms × forward+inverse
Peak scratch allocation — Newton divrem ≤ 4 × operand size Reciprocal + intermediate products
Throughput — 64-limb (4096-bit) multiply ≥ 10 M ops/sec on a single core Measured by bn-cli bench on x86_64 3 GHz
Throughput — 1024-limb (65536-bit) multiply ≥ 100 K ops/sec (NTT path) Above NTT threshold
Throughput — 64-limb modpow (e.g. RSA-4096) ≤ 50 ms per exponentiation Montgomery + sliding window
p50 latency — 4-limb add/sub ≤ 20 ns Cache-hot; no heap allocation
p50 latency — 64-limb schoolbook multiply ≤ 5 µs Single core, no SIMD
p50 latency — 1024-limb NTT multiply ≤ 5 ms Including CRT recombination
p99 latency overhead vs p50 ≤ 3× No GC pauses; allocation noise only
Startup time — bn-cli ≤ 50 ms cold Statically linked; no JIT
Shutdown time — bn-cli ≤ 5 ms Drop of Vec<Limb> buffers only
Concurrency model Single-threaded; no interior mutability All contexts (Barrett, Montgomery) are Send + Sync by value-copy
Thread safety BigUint, BigInt, BigRational are Send + Sync Immutable during sharing; mutation through owned values only
CPU assumption 64-bit word; u128 multiply without trapping Required for DoubleLimb carry capture
Cache sensitivity — small ops (≤ 8 limbs) Fit in L1 (32 KB); no cache-miss penalty Target for add/sub/compare hot path
Cache sensitivity — 256-limb ops Fit in L2 (256 KB typical); Karatsuba recursion depth ≤ 10 Threshold tuning ensures this
L3 pressure — NTT NTT butterfly buffer ≤ 8 MB for 1 M-limb operands Accept L3 misses; NTT is O(n log n) regardless
File-descriptor budget 0 (library); ≤ 3 (stdin/stdout/stderr for bn-cli) No file or network I/O in library crates
Binary size — library (release) ≤ 2 MB stripped No generics explosion; monomorphized on Limb = u64 only
Binary size — bn-cli (release, static) ≤ 5 MB Includes all algorithm crates
Storage amplification None; no persistent state All state is in-process Vec<Limb>
Availability / failure budget N/A — in-process library Panics only on programmer errors (index OOB); user errors → typed Result
Recovery objective N/A — stateless computation No checkpointing needed
Scaling model Vertical only (single thread, larger operands) Parallel multiply is PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register")
Fuzz target crash budget Zero unresolved crashes after Phase 14 cargo-fuzz run for ≥ 24 h on each harness
Conformance delta vs reference 0 discrepancies over ≥ 10 M random operand pairs Differential tests against num-bigint / Python int

4.2 Algorithm Dispatch Thresholds (Tunable Constants)

Transition Default limb threshold Rationale
Schoolbook → Karatsuba 32 limbs Below: recursion overhead exceeds savings
Karatsuba → Toom-Cook-3 128 limbs Toom-3 setup cost amortized above this size
Toom-Cook-3 → NTT 1024 limbs NTT prime setup and CRT cost amortized above this
Knuth D → Newton reciprocal 64 limbs Newton convergence overhead not worthwhile below
Schoolbook base-convert → divide-and-conquer 128 limbs D&C recursion overhead amortized above this

All thresholds are pub const values in bn-core; bn-cli bench reports measured crossover points per platform.

4.3 Memory Layout Constraints

Structure Layout Size
BigUint (n limbs) 24-byte Vec header + n × 8 bytes heap 24 + 8n bytes
BigInt Sign (1 byte + alignment) + BigUint 32 + 8n bytes
BigRational Sign + two BigUint (num, den) 56 + 8(n+d) bytes
Barrett context modulus BigUint + mu BigUint ~56 + 16k bytes for k-limb modulus
Montgomery context modulus + n_prime (Limb) + r2 BigUint ~48 + 16k bytes
DivRem two BigUint 48 + 8(q+r) bytes
NttPlan prime array (≤ 8 × u64) + roots (≤ 8 × u64) + len (u64) ≤ 144 bytes on stack

4.4 Error and Panic Policy

Condition Behavior
Division by zero Err(DivByZero) — never panics
Parse failure Err(ParseError) with byte offset of first invalid character
Subtraction underflow a - b where a < b (unsigned) Err(Underflow) or caller pre-checks; never wraps silently
Index out of bounds in limb operations Panic (programmer error, not user error)
Overflow of Vec allocation (try_reserve fails) Propagate AllocError (Phase 14 hardening)
NTT operand too large for available primes Err(NttSizeExceeded)

5. Required Reading

Books

# Title / Author / Edition Chapters to Study Concepts to Extract
B1 Knuth, The Art of Computer Programming Vol. 2: Seminumerical Algorithms, 3rd ed. (Addison-Wesley, 1997) §4.3.1 classical mult; §4.3.2 Algorithm D long division; §4.4 GCD variants; §4.5 fractions/rationals; §4.6.3 modular exp; Appendix B radix conversion Algorithm D quotient-digit estimation (qhat), two-step correction (add-back), proof of at most 2 corrections; schoolbook O(n²) baseline; Euclidean + binary GCD; modular exponentiation formulation; THE correctness reference; study proofs not just pseudocode
B2 Brent & Zimmermann, Modern Computer Arithmetic, v0.5.9 (Cambridge, 2010) — free PDF at maths.anu.edu.au §1 integer representation, add/sub; §2 multiply (Karatsuba, Toom-Cook, FFT/NTT); §3 division (Newton, Barrett, Montgomery); §5 GCD/ext-GCD (Lehmer, half-GCD); §6 base conversion (divide-and-conquer) Karatsuba split-and-recurse proof + analysis; Toom-Cook evaluation/interpolation matrices; NTT prime selection (p ≡ 1 mod 2^k); Newton convergence quadratic; Barrett mu formula; Lehmer multi-step GCD; divide-and-conquer base conversion; read end to end before writing any code
B3 Crandall & Pomerance, Prime Numbers: A Computational Perspective, 2nd ed. (Springer, 2005) §2.1–2.3 Montgomery/Barrett/modpow; §3.2 extended GCD; §9.2 NTT prime selection; §9.3 CRT reconstruction NTT prime criteria (p ≤ 30-bit so products fit u64); CRT with carry for multi-prime product recovery; Montgomery REDC pseudocode; sliding-window modpow; modular inverse methods
B4 Menezes, van Oorschot & Vanstone, Handbook of Applied Cryptography (CRC, 1997; free PDF hac.ca) §14.2 add/mul; §14.3 modular reduction (Barrett alg 14.42, Montgomery alg 14.36); §14.4 exponentiation (binary, k-ary, sliding window); §14.6 inversion Directly implementable pseudocode at the limb level; constant-time precautions noted; k-ary window selection logic; the only reference with pseudocode for Barrett and Montgomery side by side
B5 Granlund et al., GMP Manual (gmplib.org, current) Ch. 3 algorithms overview; Ch. 4 internals; Ch. 15 algorithmic index; tune/README GMP's measured thresholds for Karatsuba/Toom/FFT by CPU; mpn limb-layer contracts; heuristics for Newton vs. Knuth transition; benchmark methodology (tune/tuneup.c); do NOT copy asm or LGPL code
B6 Von zur Gathen & Gerhard, Modern Computer Algebra, 3rd ed. (Cambridge, 2013) §3 GCD + extended GCD; §5 Newton lifting; §8 Chinese Remainder Theorem CRT correctness proof; subresultant GCD vs. Lehmer; half-GCD reduction O(M(n) log n); Newton iteration for modular inversion; formal treatment of algorithm correctness
B7 Warren, Hacker's Delight, 2nd ed. (Addison-Wesley, 2013) §2 basic bit tricks; §8 divide/modulo; §11 counting bits (nlz) Hardware-level carry/borrow idioms; bit_length via leading_zeros; nlz (normalize left-shift count) used in Algorithm D's normalization shift; population count for Hamming weight
B8 Cohen, A Course in Computational Algebraic Number Theory (Springer, 1993) §1.3 multi-precision arithmetic; §1.4 GCD; §9.1 modular inverse Multi-precision GCD correctness criteria; Montgomery inverse via extended Euclidean; field arithmetic for portfoilio consumers (#92 zk-SNARK)

# Author, Year, Venue Core Contribution Implementation Relevance
P1 Karatsuba & Ofman, 1962, Doklady Akademii Nauk SSSR Divide-and-conquer integer multiply O(n^1.585) Split formula: x = x1·B + x0; middle term (x0+x1)(y0+y1) − x0y0 − x1y1; threshold where 3 recursive mults beat schoolbook; handle unequal lengths via zero-padding

6. Design Principles

Principle Summary Table

ID Name Primary Crates Key Mechanism Violated By
P1 Correctness Before Speed bn-mul, bn-div schoolbook oracle + differential tests removing schoolbook path; shipping unverified algorithm
P2 Canonical Representation bn-uint, bn-int normalize() at every op boundary returning without normalize(); skipping I1 assert
P3 No Lost Carry bn-add, bn-mul u128 carry capture using u64 wrapping_add without carry extraction
P4 Dispatch by Operand Size bn-mul, bn-div MulAlgo::select() pure function hardcoding schoolbook for all sizes; ignoring thresholds
P5 Integer-Exact Transforms bn-fftmul NTT over prime fields + CRT using f64 FFT; insufficient CRT prime coverage
P6 In-Place and Allocation-Aware bn-mul, bn-mod LimbsMut<'a> scratch; workspace Vec allocating inside modpow loop; per-iteration Vec::new()
P7 Sign and Magnitude Separate bn-int, bn-rational sign dispatch in bn-int only adding sign checks inside bn-add limb loop
P8 Division Single Contract bn-div, bn-int DivRem { a = q*b + r, 0<=r<b } returning r >= b; inconsistent floor vs. trunc in same path
P9 Constant-Time Where It Matters bn-mod subtle::CtOption; branchless select if exponent_bit branch in Montgomery loop
P10 Observability and Testability bn-mul, all pub(crate) oracle functions; inspectable dispatch #[cfg(test)]-gating schoolbook; hiding MulAlgo::select
P11 Aliasing Safety bn-add, bn-mul single-read-per-limb order; alias-detect-and-clone fallback reading a limb after overwriting it in a self-aliased in-place op
P12 Threshold Monotonicity & Tunability bn-mul MulAlgo::select() strictly ordered threshold ladder + Criterion calibration mis-ordered threshold constants; shipping unbenchmarked defaults

P1. Correctness Before Speed

Statement. Every faster algorithm (Karatsuba, Toom-Cook, NTT, Newton division) is differentially tested against the schoolbook / Knuth D baseline before being enabled in dispatch (see Phase 14).

Rationale. Arithmetic bugs compound silently — a wrong product that is 10× faster than the correct one is a defect generator for every consumer. Speed has no value without a proof of correctness against the reference.

Tradeoffs.

  • Schoolbook is O(n²) — it cannot serve as the production path; it remains a permanently-available oracle.
  • Test overhead is paid at CI time, not runtime; fuzz budget (Phase 14) is allocated specifically to cross-algorithm comparison.
  • Keeping schoolbook as pub(crate) costs code size (~2 KB) but enables runtime oracle queries.

Operational implications.

  • MulAlgo::Schoolbook is never removed from bn-mul; property tests exercise all algorithm pairs across size classes 8/32/64/256/1024/4096 limbs.
  • bn-div retains Knuth D even when Newton is the default path; both are always reachable for differential testing.
  • Feature flag differential enables side-by-side comparison in any test binary.
  • Cross-references: I4 (multiplication agreement), I6 (Newton == Knuth), I8 (NTT exactness).

Violation consequence. If Toom-Cook-3 interpolation has an off-by-one in coefficient recovery, a 1000-limb multiplication silently returns a wrong product; downstream RSA decryption produces garbage with no error signal; bug may be dormant for months.

P2. Canonical Representation

Statement. A BigUint has no trailing zero limbs; the value zero is uniquely represented as empty/zero magnitude with Sign::NoSign. Equal values have identical Vec<Limb> representations.

Rationale. Without canonicality, equality requires a full O(n) scan, hashing is undefined, and comparison by length short-circuit fails. Two non-canonical representations of the same value cause subtle bugs in GCD termination (infinite loop when gcd sees a non-zero length for zero), comparison, and Montgomery transform (wrong n_prime).

Tradeoffs.

  • Every operation that may produce trailing zeros must call normalize() before returning — cheap O(k) where k = trailing zero count.
  • The constraint is enforced at construction and at the exit of every public bn-uint function, not lazily.

Operational implications.

  • BigUint::normalize() is pub(crate); called at end of every arithmetic result constructor.
  • debug_assert!(self.is_canonical()) at every public fn entry in bn-uint.
  • Fuzzing checks normalize idempotence: normalize(normalize(x)) == normalize(x).

Violation consequence. A BigUint with a trailing zero limb appears larger by one limb; Algorithm D's quotient-digit estimate reads limb[n-1] which is zero, producing qhat = b (max value) every time, triggering unnecessary corrections and potential off-by-one in the quotient.

P3. No Lost Carry, No Undefined Overflow

Statement. Every limb-level add/mul captures carry in DoubleLimb (u128) or SignedDoubleLimb (i128 borrow). No integer overflow is implicit or relied upon; wrapping is explicit and deliberate (via Rust's wrapping_* methods with explicit carry extraction).

Rationale. Silent carry loss corrupts all higher-order limbs. In Rust, u64 overflow in release mode wraps silently — relying on this without capturing the carry is architecturally wrong even though it compiles.

Tradeoffs.

  • u128 arithmetic is slower on 32-bit targets; a Limb = u32 / DoubleLimb = u64 fallback may be needed for embedded (WASM, ARM32).
  • On 64-bit x86_64/aarch64, u128 compiles to two-register mul + carry chain; no overhead vs. native wide multiply.

Operational implications.

  • All bn-add inner loops use let hi_lo: u128 = (a as u128) + (b as u128) + (carry as u128).
  • All bn-mul inner loops use let prod: u128 = (a as u128) * (b as u128) before accumulation.
  • #![deny(arithmetic_overflow)] enabled; no u64::wrapping_add without accompanying carry extraction.

Violation consequence. A dropped carry in bn-add produces a result smaller than the true sum by exactly 2^64; invisible without oracle comparison; propagates through every downstream operation including modular exponentiation, producing silently wrong RSA signatures.

P4. Dispatch by Operand Size

Statement. Multiplication and division select a MulAlgo by limb count against measured thresholds; small operands must not pay large-algorithm overhead; large operands must not pay quadratic cost.

Rationale. Karatsuba has higher constant overhead than schoolbook; applying it to 2-limb operands is a measured regression (~3× slower). NTT has per-prime forward/inverse passes whose setup cost dominates for sub-100-limb inputs.

Tradeoffs.

  • Thresholds are platform-dependent; the library ships conservative defaults from Phase 14 calibration; users may override via BIGNUM_MUL_KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD environment variable or compile-time constant.
  • Dispatch branch overhead is O(1) vs. O(n²) compute — negligible.
  • Adding a new algorithm requires inserting a threshold constant and a benchmark to validate it.

Operational implications.

  • MulAlgo::select(n_limbs: usize, m_limbs: usize) -> MulAlgo is a pure function in bn-mul; pure = testable without arithmetic.
  • Phase 14 benchmarks validate each threshold boundary: crossing should show ≥ 10% speedup.

Violation consequence. Applying schoolbook to two 10,000-limb operands (10^8 limb multiplications) makes one RSA-4096 modpow take minutes vs. milliseconds. Applying NTT to two 3-limb operands wastes >99% of time in transform overhead.

P5. Integer-Exact Transforms

Statement. The large-multiply path (bn-fftmul) uses a number-theoretic transform over prime moduli with CRT, never floating-point FFT.

Rationale. Floating-point FFT accumulates rounding error proportional to n log n; for operands exceeding ~10,000 bits the error exceeds 1 ULP and the product is wrong by one or more limbs. NTT over prime fields has exactly zero rounding error.

Tradeoffs.

  • NTT requires primes p ≡ 1 mod 2^k (to allow primitive roots of power-of-2 orders); each prime must be ≤ 30 bits so limb × limb mod p fits in u64 without overflow.
  • Multiple primes + CRT are required to cover the full product magnitude (product of two 64-bit limb vectors needs coverage up to ~2^{128} per coefficient).
  • Implementation complexity is substantially higher than float FFT but correctness is non-negotiable (P1).

Operational implications.

  • bn-fftmul encodes NttPlan { primes: [u64; K], roots: [u64; K], len: usize } selected at construction time.
  • I8 (NTT exactness invariant) is verified by differential test against schoolbook at every CI run for inputs at the NTT threshold.
  • Floating-point types (f32, f64) are forbidden in bn-fftmul.

Violation consequence. A float FFT multiply for two 100,000-bit operands produces a result differing from the true product by 2^10 or more; every subsequent modpow, GCD, or base conversion silently corrupts data with no error signal.

P6. In-Place and Allocation-Aware

Statement. Hot kernels operate on borrowed Limbs<'a> / LimbsMut<'a> slices and reuse scratch buffers; multiplication and modpow must not allocate per inner-loop iteration.

Rationale. A 4096-bit RSA modpow calls multiply approximately 8192 times (square-and-multiply, 4096-bit exponent). Per-call heap allocation invokes the allocator 8192 times per modpow, destroying cache locality and introducing ~µs latency per call.

Tradeoffs.

  • Pre-allocated scratch requires either a caller-provided buffer or a reusable workspace owned by the operation context.
  • API surface grows (operations may need a &mut scratch parameter or a context struct).
  • Error paths must not leak scratch; Rust Drop handles this when workspace is owned by context.

Operational implications.

  • bn-mul kernels accept dst: LimbsMut, a: Limbs, b: Limbs, scratch: LimbsMut.
  • bn-mod Montgomery modpow carries workspace: Vec<Limb> that grows to high-water mark once (amortized O(1) allocation).
  • Criterion benchmarks measure alloc_count via a #[global_allocator] counting wrapper; regressions in alloc count are CI failures.

Violation consequence. A modpow that allocates per-multiply call shows non-linear scaling in benchmarks; heap fragmentation causes 10–100× latency vs. pre-allocated; observable as a benchmark cliff at the Karatsuba threshold where scratch size jumps.

P7. Sign and Magnitude Are Separate Concerns

Statement. All magnitude algorithms are unsigned and total; bn-int and bn-rational add sign rules on top without modifying limb kernels.

Rationale. Mixing sign handling into limb loops complicates carry logic and creates edge cases (double-negative, zero with non-zero sign). Clean layering isolates magnitude bugs and prevents sign-layer bugs from corrupting limbs.

Tradeoffs.

  • Sign dispatch in bn-int (choose which magnitude to subtract from which, based on comparison) requires one O(n) comparison call per signed add/sub — inexpensive relative to the arithmetic itself.
  • Two's-complement bitwise ops on BigInt (AND/OR/XOR) require synthesizing sign extension, which is handled symbolically in bn-int without modifying bn-uint.

Operational implications.

  • bn-add, bn-mul, bn-div functions accept Limbs / BigUint, never BigInt.
  • bn-int's add(a: &BigInt, b: &BigInt) dispatches to: (same sign) magnitude add; (opposite sign) compare magnitudes then subtract smaller from larger; sign assigned after.
  • No sign logic anywhere in bn-add, bn-mul, bn-div, bn-mod, bn-gcd.

Violation consequence. Embedding sign in bn-add requires special-casing zero, positive, and negative in every carry loop; a missed case (0 - x forgetting to negate) corrupts result sign silently; all downstream signed arithmetic produces wrong sign without error.

P8. Division Has a Single Contract

Statement. Every divrem(a, b) call satisfies a = q × b + r with 0 ≤ r < b (BigUint); signed BigInt exposes both div_trunc/rem_trunc (toward zero) and div_floor/mod_floor (Python-compatible), each with its own documented rounding convention.

Rationale. Multiple incompatible rounding conventions in one codebase cause silent consumer bugs (e.g., negative modular residue passed to Montgomery). One contract per function, stated once, forces callers to adapt rather than guess.

Tradeoffs.

  • Both rounding variants must be implemented and tested independently in bn-int.
  • Default operator % maps to rem_trunc (Rust convention); mod_floor is an explicit call.
  • DivByZero is the only error; panic is reserved for programming errors (internal assertions).

Operational implications.

  • DivByZero typed error returned, never unwrap or panic in bn-div public paths.
  • I5 property: ∀ a, b ≠ 0 : a == q*b + r ∧ 0 ≤ r < b tested by proptest with auto-shrinking.
  • Division by single limb is a separate fast path in bn-div (avoids full Algorithm D for b < 2^64).

Violation consequence. A divrem returning r = b (missed correction step) breaks the GCD algorithm's termination invariant (Euclidean step requires r < b); infinite loop or panic in bn-gcd for inputs that trigger the off-by-one.

P9. Constant-Time Where It Matters

Statement. Modular exponentiation in bn-mod offers a constant-time path (data-independent branching, data-independent memory access pattern) selectable via a ConstantTime marker; timing variation on that path is a security defect, not a performance tradeoff.

Rationale. Square-and-multiply timing attacks on RSA/ECDH require only ~1000 network-observable oracle queries to recover a 1024-bit private key if exponent bits control branches.

Tradeoffs.

  • Constant-time windowed modpow is ~15–30% slower than the variable-time variant.
  • The performance path (Variable marker) remains available for non-crypto use (factorial, base conversion, primality testing).
  • User selects via marker types; incorrect use of Variable on a private key is a caller bug, not a library bug.

Operational implications.

  • Constant-time path uses subtle::CtOption, subtle::ConditionallySelectable, and branchless limb select throughout bn-mod.
  • Reviewed against Bernstein constant-time guidelines (no secret-dependent branches, no secret-dependent memory indices).
  • CI runs valgrind --tool=memcheck --track-origins=yes on a test harness that marks the exponent as VALGRIND_MAKE_MEM_UNDEFINED to detect secret-dependent branches.

Violation consequence. A single if exponent_bit == 1 { square_and_multiply() } branch in the Montgomery loop leaks one bit per modpow call; 1024 adaptive timing observations recover a 1024-bit private key via square-and-multiply timing; this is CVE-class.

P10. Observability and Testability

Statement. Algorithm selection, operand sizes, and iteration counts are inspectable at runtime and at test time; the schoolbook reference is permanently accessible as an oracle for any magnitude operation.

Rationale. A library that hides its algorithm selection cannot be debugged, benchmarked, or fuzz-verified. When NTT gives a wrong result for a specific input size, the engineer must reproduce the failure with schoolbook to isolate whether the bug is in CRT, the NTT kernel, or the dispatch.

Tradeoffs.

  • Schoolbook staying as pub(crate) (not #[cfg(test)]) adds ~2 KB to the final binary.
  • Inspectable dispatch is a minor abstraction leak — it exposes internal thresholds as observable behavior.
  • Both are acceptable given the correctness-first mandate (P1).

Operational implications.

  • MulAlgo::select(n, m) is pub for benchmarks and external threshold tuning tools.
  • bn-mul exposes mul_schoolbook, mul_karatsuba, mul_toom3, mul_ntt as pub(crate) direct-call entry points.
  • Phase 14 fuzz corpus includes a differential oracle target for each algorithm pair.
  • Criterion benchmarks expose per-algorithm timings at each size class.

Violation consequence. Without an oracle, a Toom-Cook bug in coefficient C3 recovery is invisible until a consumer (RSA key generation) silently produces a composite modulus presented as prime; the bug can lie dormant across thousands of key generations before manifesting as a decryption failure.

P11. Aliasing Safety

Statement. Every in-place arithmetic entry point (add_assign, sub_assign, mul_assign, and their LimbsMut-level kernels in bn-add/bn-mul) produces the correct result when the destination aliases a source operand — op(&mut a, &a) must equal op(&mut a, &a.clone()) — even though the kernel reads and writes the same backing storage.

Rationale. Rust's borrow checker prevents &mut a and &a from coexisting as distinct bindings at a single call site, but self-referential operations are still reachable through owning wrapper methods: a += &a, a.square_in_place(), and the repeated squaring step inside every modpow's exponentiation ladder all pass the same BigUint as both destination and source. A limb loop that overwrites a[i] before a later step needs to read the original a[i] (e.g. Karatsuba's multi-pass split-recombine, which reads low and high halves of a after already writing partial products back into the same buffer) silently corrupts its own input.

Tradeoffs.

  • Detecting aliasing (comparing the underlying slice pointers) costs one comparison per call — negligible next to the O(n) or O(n²) kernel cost.
  • Read-once kernels (schoolbook add/sub: each a[i] is read exactly once to produce its own replacement) are alias-safe by construction and need no clone; multi-pass kernels (Karatsuba, Toom-Cook split/recombine) require an alias-detect-and-clone fallback, which costs one extra allocation only on the rare self-aliased call path.
  • Alternative rejected: forbid aliasing entirely by always cloning on entry — this would silently violate P6 (allocation-aware) for the common case of squaring in a modpow inner loop, which is exactly the path most sensitive to per-call allocation cost.

Operational implications.

  • add_limbs_in_place(a: LimbsMut<'_>, b: Limbs<'_>) and sub_assign's inner loop iterate so that each output limb is written only after its own read is complete, making bn-add's in-place path alias-safe without a fallback.
  • bn-mul's Karatsuba/Toom-Cook in-place variants detect a.as_ptr() == b.as_ptr() (or dst overlapping a/b) and route through a clone-then-op fallback before recursing; the schoolbook and NTT paths, which only read a/b before writing dst, do not need the check.
  • BigUint::square_in_place(&mut self) and MulAssign<&BigUint> for BigUint when rhs points at self both dispatch through the aliasing-safe path, never through a raw multi-pass kernel directly.
  • A differential aliasing property test — op(&mut a, &a) == op(a.clone(), a.clone()) for add, sub, and mul — runs in bn-add/tests and bn-mul/tests across all dispatch algorithms (I11).

Violation consequence. If Karatsuba's in-place variant reads x1 from a buffer region it has already overwritten with a partial product, the result silently diverges from schoolbook(a, a) only when the caller passes the same BigUint as both operands — a call pattern common enough to occur on every modpow squaring step, yet rare enough in a naive test suite (which typically multiplies two distinct values) to ship undetected until a production RSA modpow returns a wrong result.

P12. Threshold Monotonicity & Tunability

Statement. The multiply dispatch ladder — schoolbook → Karatsuba → Toom-Cook-3 → NTT — is strictly ordered by increasing crossover size (KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD < TOOM3_THRESHOLD < NTT_THRESHOLD), each threshold is independently configurable (compile-time constant, overridable via a BN_*_THRESHOLD mechanism per P4), and every threshold boundary is validated by a benchmark showing the more complex algorithm is faster on its side of the boundary.

Rationale. The four algorithms agree on the answer (P1, I4) but differ in constant factor and asymptotic complexity; only a correctly ordered, calibrated ladder guarantees the dispatch is also fast. A threshold that is present but wrong — e.g. TOOM3_THRESHOLD accidentally set below KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD — does not corrupt results, it silently selects a slower path (Toom-Cook's higher constant overhead applied to operands too small to amortize it) or an under-benchmarked one (dispatch falling through to NTT for sizes the NTT prime set was never measured against).

Tradeoffs.

  • Asserting KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD < TOOM3_THRESHOLD < NTT_THRESHOLD at compile time or module init costs one comparison chain, paid once, not per call.
  • Per-platform recalibration (GMP tune/tuneup.c methodology, B5) is manual; the library ships conservative Phase 14 defaults rather than auto-tuning at install time, trading peak performance on unusual hardware for reproducible builds (ADR-25).
  • Exposing thresholds as pub for override (P10) means a misconfigured downstream override is a caller error, not a library defect — the library still documents and tests the required ordering even when a caller overrides individual values.

Operational implications.

  • MulAlgo::select(n_limbs, m_limbs) -> MulAlgo in bn-mul/src/dispatch.rs is the single source of truth for the ordering; module init asserts KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD < TOOM3_THRESHOLD and TOOM3_THRESHOLD < NTT_THRESHOLD.
  • Phase 14's Criterion suite (bench_mul.rs) sweeps limb sizes across every boundary ±10% and reports whether the algorithm on the larger side of each threshold is actually faster; a regression here is a P1-severity performance issue in the Runbooks even though it is not a correctness bug.
  • dispatch_tests.rs (Phase 5) confirms the MulAlgo variant selected at, just below, and just above each threshold, restating the monotonicity requirement as a unit test.

Violation consequence. If a threshold-tuning edit swaps the KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD and TOOM3_THRESHOLD constants, MulAlgo::select starts routing larger operands to Karatsuba past the point where Toom-Cook would be faster, and smaller operands to Toom-Cook where its higher constant overhead makes it slower than Karatsuba — every multiply in the crossed range still returns the correct product (P1/I4 still hold), but silently regresses performance 20–40%, the kind of defect that passes every correctness test in CI and surfaces only when someone watches the benchmark trend line.

7. System Invariants

Invariant Overview

ID Short Name Type Phase Introduced Crate Scope

I2 Sign canonicalization structural Phase 9 bn-int, bn-rational

I5 Division identity contract Phase 7 bn-div

I7 Modular range contract Phase 10 bn-mod
I8 NTT exactness algorithmic Phase 6 bn-fftmul

I10 Rational canonicality structural Phase 13 bn-rational
I11 In-place aliasing correctness algorithmic Phase 2 bn-add, bn-mul
