Performance Engineering

Performance Metrics Register

ID Metric Target Phase Gate
M1 64-limb (4096-bit) schoolbook multiply throughput ≥ 10 M ops/sec, single core, x86_64 3 GHz Phase 4
M2 1024-limb (65536-bit) NTT-path multiply throughput ≥ 100 K ops/sec Phase 6
M3 RSA-4096 (4096-bit exponent) Montgomery modpow latency ≤ 50 ms per exponentiation Phase 11
M4 4-limb add/sub p50 latency ≤ 20 ns, cache-hot, no heap allocation Phase 2
M5 64-limb schoolbook multiply p50 latency ≤ 5 µs, single core, no SIMD Phase 4
M6 1024-limb NTT multiply p50 latency ≤ 5 ms, including CRT recombination Phase 6
M7 p99/p50 latency overhead ratio ≤ 3×, no GC pauses, allocation noise only Phase 14
M8 bn-cli cold startup time ≤ 50 ms, statically linked, no JIT Phase 14
M9 NTT multiply peak scratch allocation ≤ 6 × padded transform length (limbs) Phase 6
M10 Newton-reciprocal divrem peak scratch allocation ≤ 4 × operand size Phase 8
M11 Single-operand memory ceiling ≤ 8 MB (1 M limbs × 8 bytes) Phase 1
M12 Library binary size (release, stripped) ≤ 2 MB, monomorphized on Limb = u64 only Phase 14

Benchmarking Strategy

All benchmarks use Criterion 0.5+ with measurement_time = 10s, sample_size = 100.

Benchmark group Inputs Metric
mul/schoolbook 1,2,4,8,16-limb pairs ns/op
mul/karatsuba 16,32,64,128-limb pairs ns/op
mul/toom3 128,256,512-limb pairs ns/op
mul/ntt 1024,4096,16384-limb pairs ns/op
mul/dispatch all threshold boundary ±1 ns/op; catches threshold-straddling regressions
div/knuth 64,256,1024-limb dividend / 32,128-limb divisor ns/op
div/newton same as knuth ns/op; must undercut Knuth above ~256 limbs
modpow/montgomery_2048 RSA-2048 exponent ms/op
modpow/montgomery_4096 RSA-4096 exponent ms/op
gcd/binary 1024-limb pairs ns/op
gcd/lehmer 1024-limb pairs ns/op
convert/to_decimal 1000-digit, 100k-digit integers ms/op
convert/from_decimal 1000-digit, 100k-digit strings ms/op

Throughput targets derived from Phase 4 (schoolbook baseline):

Operation Input size Target throughput
Schoolbook multiply 64×64-limb ≥500 MB/s of limb data
Karatsuba multiply 256×256-limb ≥3× schoolbook ops/s
Toom-Cook-3 512×512-limb ≥5× schoolbook ops/s
NTT multiply 4096×4096-limb super-linear; ≥10× Toom ops/s
Knuth divrem 512-limb / 256-limb within 2× of schoolbook multiply cost
Montgomery modpow 2048-bit ≤50 ms on Tier-1 x86_64

Profiling Methodology

  • Flamegraphs: cargo flamegraph --bench mul via perf record (Linux) or Instruments (macOS); examine hot loops in limb-kernel functions.
  • DHAT (heap profiler): cargo +nightly test --features dhat-heap with DHAT overhead output; check inner-loop allocation count is zero (P6).
  • Callgrind: valgrind --tool=callgrind for instruction-count determinism; useful for cross-machine comparison without timing noise.
  • perf stat counter set: cycles, instructions, cache-misses, branch-misses, LLC-load-misses.

Flamegraph Workflow

  1. cargo flamegraph --bench mul/ntt -- --bench → produces flamegraph.svg.
  2. Identify: expected hot frames are ntt_forward, pointwise_mul, ntt_inverse, crt_recombine.
  3. Unexpected hotspots: Vec allocations in inner NTT loop indicate missing buffer reuse (P6 violation).
  4. Save flamegraph to benches/flamegraphs/ per release tag.

Tracing Methodology

  • tracing crate with RUST_LOG=bn_mul=trace emits algorithm selection events (MulAlgo chosen, limb counts).
  • CI benchmark runs with --features trace-algo to verify threshold dispatch (P4 correctness).
  • No tracing overhead in release builds without the feature flag.

Allocator Analysis

Tool Target What to find
DHAT modpow inner loop Zero heap allocations per Montgomery REDC step
Valgrind massif 4096-limb multiply Peak RSS ≤ 3× input size
custom counting allocator (test-only) Karatsuba recursion Allocation count O(log n) not O(n)

Cache Analysis

  • Schoolbook inner loop: limb operands ≤ 16 KB fit in L1 (512 limbs × 8B each = 4 KB).
  • Karatsuba splits at ~128 limbs → sub-problems fit in L1.
  • Toom-Cook-3 splits at ~256 limbs → sub-problems fit in L2.
  • NTT working set: 2 × padded length × 8B; plan NTT chunk size so transform buffer fits in L3.
  • Cache-miss target: <1% LLC miss rate on multiply benchmarks measured by perf stat.

Memory Profiling

  • Track peak RSS during bn-cli factorial 100000; must not exceed 256 MB.
  • Verify no monotonic RSS growth during soak test (allocator stability).
  • heaptrack optional for detailed allocation site attribution.

Tail-Latency Analysis

  • Criterion's default reports p50/p95/p99; ensure p99/p50 < 1.5× (no GC or OS jitter spikes in library code).
  • If p99 is an outlier, use criterion --warm-up-time 5 to separate JIT/page-fault effects from steady-state.

Scalability Analysis

Dominant Scaling Pressures

This is a pure-compute, single-process arithmetic library. There are no network, database, or concurrency concerns. The dominant scaling axis is operand size in limbs (n).

Concern Scaling law Ceiling
Schoolbook multiply O(n²) Becomes unusable above ~64 limbs; must dispatch by Phase 5
Karatsuba multiply O(n^1.585) Dominant 64–512 limbs
Toom-Cook-3 O(n^1.465) Dominant 256–4096 limbs
NTT multiply O(n log n log log n) Practical ceiling: ~10^6 limbs (8 MB operand)
Knuth Algorithm D O(n²) Transition to Newton above ~256 limbs
Newton-reciprocal div O(M(n)) where M = best multiply Scales with multiplication; no separate quadratic term
Lehmer GCD O(n²) schoolbook ops Acceptable to ~4096 limbs; beyond: sub-quadratic GCD (out of scope)
Base-10 conversion schoolbook O(n²) digit ops Transitions to D&C at ~100 limbs
Base-10 conversion D&C O(M(n) log n) Scales with multiplication

Algorithm Threshold Calibration

Thresholds are hardware-measured, not hard-coded constants:

Transition Approximate limb count Tuning mechanism
Schoolbook → Karatsuba ~32–64 MUL_KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD in bn-mul
Karatsuba → Toom-Cook-3 ~128–256 MUL_TOOM3_THRESHOLD
Toom-Cook-3 → NTT ~2048–4096 MUL_NTT_THRESHOLD
Knuth → Newton division ~128–256 DIV_NEWTON_THRESHOLD
Schoolbook → D&C base-conv ~100 digits CONVERT_DC_THRESHOLD

Thresholds should be validated on the CI tier (x86_64 Linux). Separate threshold tables may be compiled for aarch64.

Memory Scaling

Operation Peak memory (n = limb count)
Schoolbook a×b Output: n+m limbs; scratch: 1 DoubleLimb (register)
Karatsuba O(n log n) scratch via recursive allocation
Toom-Cook-3 7 sub-product buffers, each ≤ n limbs
NTT 3 prime-modulus arrays of padded length 2n; ≈ 6n×8B
Knuth D Scratch equal to divisor length for normalization
Newton div Reciprocal buffer n limbs + multiply scratch
Montgomery modpow Window table: 2^w × n limbs (w=4 → 16 Montgomery elements)

RSS growth is O(n) for all operations except Karatsuba recursion (O(n log n) stack+heap).

Vertical Scaling (Single Core)

  • All kernels are single-threaded; no rayon or std::thread.
  • SIMD: not used in initial implementation; optional SIMD limb-loop paths guarded by #[cfg(target_feature="avx2")] may be added in Phase 14.
  • Clock-speed scaling: operations are memory-bandwidth-limited (NTT) or compute-limited (schoolbook); both benefit linearly from frequency.
  • L3 cache size determines the NTT operand size that runs near peak; beyond L3 the transform becomes memory-bound.

Horizontal Scaling

  • The library is a pure function library; parallelism is the caller's responsibility.
  • Independent modular exponentiations (RSA batch signing) parallelize trivially via rayon::par_iter at the call site.
  • No shared mutable state; no Mutex; each BigUint is independently owned.
  • Multi-threaded NTT (splitting the butterfly passes across threads) is out of scope for Phase 6 but possible without API changes.

Scaling Ceilings and Practical Limits

Limit Value Reason
Maximum operand (NTT) ~10^6 limbs ≈ 64 Mbit NTT prime product must exceed the output magnitude
Maximum NTT prime set 3 primes, product ≥ 2^192 Sufficient for 64-bit limbs × 10^6 element products
Vec<Limb> length usize::MAX / 8 (platform pointer) Not a practical limit
Stack depth (Karatsuba) log2(n) frames ≈ 20 for n=10^6 Each frame ~100 bytes; ~2 KB stack; safe
Stack depth (D&C convert) log2(n) ≈ 20 frames Same analysis
Modpow window size w 4–6 bits; larger wastes Montgomery table 2^6 = 64 precomputed values; 512 limbs each at 4096-bit

Coordination Overhead

None. The library has no locks, no channels, no async, no global mutable state. The only shared resource is the system allocator; under high parallelism, allocator contention may appear. Mitigation: reuse buffers (P6) to reduce alloc/free rate.

Metadata Scaling

  • No registry, no map, no runtime dispatch table. MulAlgo dispatch is a single integer comparison.
  • NttPlan is stack-allocated per call; no caching required.
  • Barrett/Montgomery contexts are caller-managed structs; creation cost is O(n) and is amortized over many reductions.

Mental Models & First Principles

  • MM1 — A bignum is a positional number in base 2⁶⁴; a limb is one digit. Add/sub/mul are grade-school algorithms whose load-bearing detail is carry capture in u128. Predicts: correctness ≡ never dropping the high half of a limb×limb product. Bug without it: truncating the carry word → silently wrong high digits. Applied: §8, I3, Phase 2/4.
  • MM2 — Multiplication cost is superlinear, so the algorithm must change with size. Schoolbook O(n²) → Karatsuba O(n¹·⁵⁸⁵) → Toom-3 O(n¹·⁴⁶) → NTT O(n log n). Predicts: any single fixed algorithm is wrong somewhere on the size axis. Bug without it: schoolbook for 100K-limb operands → quadratic blow-up. Applied: §4.2 thresholds, Phases 4–6, ADR-10.
  • MM3 — NTT is the FFT done in a finite field, so the transform is exact. Convolution becomes pointwise multiply in the transform domain; CRT recombines three prime channels. Predicts: the prime product must exceed the max coefficient or digits wrap silently. Bug without it: prime product too small → wrong result with no error. Applied: I8, Phase 6, ADR-11.
  • MM4 — Division is multiplication's expensive inverse; Newton converts it back into multiplications. A reciprocal iteration doubles its correct digits each step. Predicts: above a threshold, divide via reciprocal, not subtract-and-shift. Bug without it: O(n²) Knuth D on huge quotients. Applied: Phases 7–8, §4.2, MM2.
  • MM5 — Modular reduction is crypto's hot loop: precompute once, reduce many times. Barrett μ or Montgomery (n′, r²) is built per modulus and reused. Predicts: recomputing per operation dominates modpow cost. Bug without it: rebuilding the Montgomery context inside the exponentiation loop. Applied: P6, ADR-12, Phases 10–11.
  • MM6 — Sign and magnitude are separable. Do all hard arithmetic on unsigned magnitudes; layer sign rules on top. Predicts: unsigned subtraction where a < b must be detected, not wrapped. Bug without it: silent wraparound on underflow. Applied: §4.4, Phase 9.
  • MM7 — Constant-time is a property of control flow and memory access, not of the math. A data-dependent branch or table index leaks the secret. Predicts: variable-time modpow is faster but unsafe for keys. Bug without it: secret-dependent window lookup leaks exponent bits. Applied: P9, ADR-13, Phase 11.
  • MM8 — #![forbid(unsafe_code)] + u128 lets the optimizer emit carry instructions without hand-written unsafe. Predicts: GMP-class speed is reachable on the dominant paths without unsafe. Bug without it: introducing UB in a hand-rolled limb kernel for marginal gain. Applied: ADR-26, P1/P4.

Comparative Architecture

Dimension This design (bn) GMP (mpn/mpz) OpenSSL BIGNUM Java BigInteger CPython int (PyLong) libtommath
Limb width u64 (u128 carry) mp_limb_t 32/64 + asm BN_ULONG word int[] base 2³² 30-bit digits in array mp_digit (configurable)
Multiply ladder school/Karatsuba/Toom3/NTT school/Karatsuba/Toom(2..8.5)/FFT school/Karatsuba school/Karatsuba/Toom3 school/Karatsuba school/Karatsuba/Toom3
Division Knuth D / Newton reciprocal Knuth + divide-and-conquer Knuth Knuth D Knuth Knuth
Modular reduction Barrett + Montgomery Montgomery REDC + asm Montgomery (BN_MONT_CTX) Montgomery Montgomery-style pow Montgomery + Barrett
Constant-time opt-in feature mostly variable-time BN_FLG_CONSTTIME paths none none limited
Implementation safe Rust, no unsafe per-arch assembly C + asm Java (immutable) C portable C
  • GMP made raw speed the single objective — per-microarchitecture assembly and a Schönhage–Strassen FFT path push it to the asymptotic frontier — it wins whenever absolute throughput on huge operands is all that matters and portability/auditability are secondary.
  • OpenSSL BIGNUM made constant-time a first-class flag (BN_FLG_CONSTTIME) because it lives inside TLS — it wins where every modular op may touch a private key and side-channel resistance outranks peak speed.
  • Java BigInteger chose JVM portability and immutability (each op allocates a fresh object) — it wins where GC-managed safety and write-once value semantics matter more than allocation cost.
  • CPython int uses 30-bit digits in a 32-bit array so a carry never overflows a C int, and the same code runs everywhere — it wins for maximal portability and seamless small-int↔bignum transitions in a dynamic language.
  • libtommath optimizes for portability and embeddability (configurable digit size, no asm, public domain) — it wins in constrained/embedded crypto where a small, dependency-free, auditable C file beats a tuned monolith.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

All inputs are taken from §4 (System Constraints / Memory Layout / Dispatch Thresholds); arithmetic is shown.

  1. Limbs for a k-bit number. limbs = ceil(k/64). RSA-4096 → 4096/64 = 64 limbs (matches §4 "64-limb (4096-bit)").
  2. BigUint heap bytes. 24 (Vec header) + 8n (§4.3). 1M limbs → 24 + 8e6 ≈ 8 MB (matches §4 memory ceiling).
  3. Schoolbook 64×64 mul. n·m = 64·64 = 4096 u128 partial products; at ~1 product/ns → ~4 µs ≈ §4 p50 ≤ 5 µs.
  4. Schoolbook scratch. ≤ 2(n+m) = 2·128 = 256 limbs × 8 = 2 KB → L1-resident (§4 cache row).
  5. Karatsuba crossover. schoolbook n² vs Karatsuba ≈ 3(n/2)^1.585; equal near n ≈ 32 limbs (matches §4.2 threshold).
  6. 1024-limb NTT. O(n log n) × 3 primes × (fwd+inv) ≈ 6 · 1024 · log₂2048 ≈ 6·1024·11 ≈ 67.6K butterflies × tens of ns → ~5 ms (matches §4 p50).
  7. NTT scratch. ≤ 6 × padded length (§4). 1024 → pad 2048 → 6·2048·8 = 98 KB → L2/L3 resident depending on size.
  8. modpow REDC count. e-bit exponent, window w=5 → ≈ e squarings + e/(w+1) muls = 4096 + 683 ≈ 4779 modmuls; each 64-limb modmul ≈ 5 µs → ≈ 24 ms < 50 ms budget (§4).
  9. Montgomery context size. ≈ 48 + 16k for k-limb modulus (§4.3). k=64 → ≈ 1.1 KB, built once, amortized over ~4779 REDCs (P6).
  10. Toom-3 vs Karatsuba. Toom-3 = 5 sub-products of n/3 limbs vs Karatsuba 3 of n/2; setup (5-point eval + interpolation) pushes crossover to 128 limbs (§4.2).
  11. Decimal length. n-limb number ≈ n · log₁₀(2⁶⁴) ≈ n · 19.27 decimal digits; D&C base-convert is O(M(n) log n) above 128 limbs (§4.2).
  12. Binary size. monomorphized on Limb=u64 only → no generic explosion → ≤ 2 MB stripped library (§4).

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

  1. Why must the multiply path switch algorithms with operand size (§4.2, MM2), and how do you derive the Karatsuba-beats-schoolbook crossover (Capacity Model)?
  2. NTT uses three primes + CRT (Phase 6, ADR-11). What silently breaks if their product is below the max coefficient (I8), and how does §4 bound operand size to prevent it?
  3. Why is Newton-reciprocal division (Phase 8) asymptotically better than Knuth D (Phase 7) yet slower below 64 limbs (§4.2, MM4)?
  4. Quantify the cost blow-up if the Montgomery context were rebuilt per REDC inside modpow (Capacity Model, P6, Phase 11).
  5. Why does unsigned subtraction return Err(Underflow) rather than wrap (§4.4, MM6), and how does the signed layer (Phase 9) build on that?
  6. What exactly makes the ct-modpow path constant-time (P9, ADR-13, MM7), and which two leak channels does the default variable-time path expose?
  7. Why is #![forbid(unsafe_code)] compatible with GMP-class performance on the dominant paths (ADR-26, MM8, P4)?
  8. At what operand size do the 8 MB single-operand ceiling and the 6×-transform-length NTT scratch bound collide (§4, Capacity Model)?
  9. Why are limbs little-endian (ADR-06), and how does that interact with add/sub scan direction and Vec resize cost (MM1, Phase 2)?
  10. Why is differential testing against num-bigint and Python int (ADR-16, §4 gate) the right oracle, and what bug class would a hand-written unit test miss?
  11. Derive the limb count and modmul count for RSA-4096 exponentiation (Capacity Model) and show why it fits the 50 ms budget (§4).
  12. Why does the library forbid serde in core crates (ADR-07) yet expose Display/from_str in bn-convert (Phase 12) — what compile-graph property is protected?
  13. Why does Toom-Cook-3's setup cost push its crossover above Karatsuba's (Phase 5, §4.2, Capacity Model)?
  14. Why must NTT be integer-exact rather than a floating FFT (I8, MM3), and what does choosing primes ≡ 1 (mod 2ᵏ) buy you (ADR-11)?
  15. Why are all contexts Send + Sync by value-copy with no interior mutability (§4, ADR-14), and what concurrency hazard does that eliminate?
  16. Why is schoolbook-oracle agreement for ≤ 512 limbs (§4, I4) the load-bearing invariant of the entire multiply ladder (Phases 4–6)?
  17. Where does precision/cancellation risk appear in a pure integer library (contrast #401 soft-float, §2.5), and why does its absence simplify NTT correctness vs a float FFT?
  18. Why is the limb width u64 with u128 carry (ADR-05, MM1) rather than u32, and how does that halve loop iteration counts (Capacity Model)?
  19. What is the un-defer trigger for parallel (Rayon) multiply (Deferred Register, §4 scaling model), and why is it deferred rather than core?
  20. Why does constant-time modpow ship behind a feature flag rather than as the default (ADR-13, P9), and what does the non-crypto consumer save?

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

A1 — Fixed-width / stack-inlined limbs (small-size optimization).

[u64; K]  ── on stack, no heap ── add/mul over fixed K limbs

Wins for crypto with known modulus sizes (256/4096-bit) where heap allocation dominates and operands never grow. Embodied by fixed-width crates (crypto-bigint, EVM U256). Rejected because bn is arbitrary-precision by mandate (§1); a fixed cap defeats the purpose (an SSO fast path could be added later, not as the core).

A2 — Schönhage–Strassen FFT over Z/(2ⁿ+1) (single transform, no CRT).

operands → negacyclic transform mod 2^n+1 → pointwise mul → inverse

Wins at extreme sizes (millions of digits) where SS's O(n log n log log n) and the absence of CRT recombination pay off. Embodied by GMP's FFT path. Rejected because three-prime NTT is simpler to keep exact and target sizes (≤ 1M limbs) sit below the SS advantage (Phase 6).

A3 — Residue Number System (RNS).

x → (x mod p1, x mod p2, … )  ── add/mul fully parallel per channel

Wins when add/mul are the only operations and they can run parallel/SIMD per residue (DSP, some FHE backends). Embodied by RNS-CKKS. Rejected because comparison, division, and base conversion are expensive in RNS, and this library must support all of them (Phases 7, 12).

A4 — GMP FFI wrapper (link libgmp, safe Rust facade).

Rust API → unsafe FFI → libgmp (mpz_*) → result

Wins when you only need the fastest possible bignum and don't care how it's built. Embodied by the rug crate. Rejected because the project's purpose is from-scratch mastery (§1, ADR-04); wrapping GMP bypasses every lesson.

Deferred Features Register

Feature Anchor Building Phase Un-defer Trigger
no_std / alloc-only target Deferred Features Register post-v1 portability phase an embedded consumer needs the library without std
SIMD / AVX-512 limb kernels Deferred Features Register post-v1 perf phase limb add/mul loops dominate profiles and SIMD shows measured gain
serde serialization Deferred Features Register post-v1 interop phase a consumer needs structured/zero-copy (de)serialization
Parallel multiply (Rayon) Deferred Features Register post-v1 perf phase NTT/Toom multiply of huge operands is wall-clock-bound on multicore
Decimal-float overlay Deferred Features Register post-v1 numeric phase a use case requires arbitrary-precision decimal floating point

Glossary

45 terms. Alphabetical. Each entry: term, precise implementation-relevant definition, cross-references to crate/module, phase, invariant, or principle. Prefer a definition list for line-density.

Add-back Correction in Knuth Algorithm D (Phase 7, bn-div/src/knuth_d.rs): after multiply-subtract, if the subtraction underflows, add the divisor back once and decrement the quotient digit by one.

  • Frequency: ≤ 2/b per digit step; rare but mandatory to handle.
  • Omitting it produces an incorrect quotient with no other error signal.

Arbitrary precision Integer magnitude bounded only by heap memory; BigUint limb count (Vec<Limb>) grows on demand.

  • Contrast: fixed-width u64/u128; BigUint has no ceiling on representable value.

Asymptotic threshold Limb count at which a faster-asymptotic algorithm crosses below the simpler one in wall-clock cost.

  • KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD, TOOM3_THRESHOLD in bn-mul/src/thresholds.rs; tuned by benches/mul_bench.rs (Phase 5, P4).
  • NTT threshold in bn-fftmul/src/lib.rs; tuned by benches/mul_bench.rs for the NTT crossover (Phase 6).

Barrett reduction Precomputed modular reduction: mu = floor(R^(2k) / m) computed once per modulus.

  • Reduction: a mod m ≈ a - m * floor(a * mu / R^(2k)); at most two correction subtractions.
  • No trial division; suitable for repeated mod with the same modulus (bn-mod/src/barrett.rs, Phase 10, I7).
  • Reference: Barrett 1986 (CRYPTO '86).

Bezout coefficients Integers u, v satisfying a*u + b*v = gcd(a,b).

  • Produced by extended GCD (bn-gcd/src/ext_gcd.rs); u and v may be negative → typed as BigInt.
  • Direct use: mod_inv when modulus is composite (Phase 11).

Bignum Variable-length integer representation; here: BigUint (unsigned magnitude) or BigInt (sign + magnitude).

  • Lineage: GMP mpz_t, Java BigInteger, Python int; this library re-implements from scratch in Rust.

Binary GCD (Stein's algorithm) GCD computation using shifts and subtractions instead of multi-precision division.

  • Step: factor out common powers of 2; alternate halving the even operand and subtracting (bn-gcd/src/binary.rs, Phase 11).
  • Complexity: O(n^2) bit operations; simpler but slower than Lehmer for large inputs.

Borrow Signed underflow in limb-wise subtraction; captured in SignedDoubleLimb = i128; propagated to the next limb.

  • sub in bn-add requires a >= b as a stated precondition; borrow never dropped (I3).

Canonical form Uniqueness invariant: equal values have bit-identical representations.

  • BigUint: no trailing zero limbs; zero is the empty or single-zero limb vector (I1).
  • BigInt: Sign == NoSign iff magnitude is zero (I2); never Plus or Minus for zero.
  • BigRational: gcd(num, den) == 1; den > 0; sign carried on the Sign field (I10).
  • Consequence: equality and hashing are O(n) limb comparisons, not algebraic normalization.

Carry Unsigned overflow on limb addition or multiply-accumulate; high 64 bits of a u128 intermediate.

  • Propagated to the next limb index in the loop; never dropped (I3).
  • Result length increases by at most one limb on add (Phase 2).

Chinese Remainder Theorem (CRT) Reconstruction from residues in bn-fftmul/src/crt.rs (Phase 6).

  • NTT multiplies modulo several independent primes; CRT recovers the unique integer in [0, prod(primes)).
  • Enables integer-exact large multiplication without floating-point (P5, I8).

Constant-time Execution time and branch pattern independent of secret data values.

  • Required for modular exponentiation path used in cryptographic contexts (P9).
  • Implementation: non-branching conditional selection; fixed-size window scan; table-based power lookup in bn-mod/src/modpow.rs.
  • Timing independence verified by test comparing constant-time vs. variable-time output; absence of secret-dependent branches checked by inspection.

Cooley-Tukey butterfly NTT algorithm in bn-fftmul/src/ntt.rs (Phase 6): recursively splits length-n transform into two length-n/2 sub-transforms; combines with twiddle factors (powers of the root of unity).

  • Preceded by bit-reversal permutation; O(n log n) multiplications in Z_p.

Differential testing Primary correctness strategy (P1, P10, Phase 14): compare two algorithm implementations over random operands across all size classes; slow-but-correct variant is the oracle.

  • Karatsuba, Toom-Cook, NTT each compared against schoolbook (I4).
  • Newton-reciprocal compared against Knuth D (I6).
  • Full library compared against num-bigint crate and Python int in Phase 14 conformance.

Divide-and-conquer base conversion Algorithm in bn-convert/src/dc_conv.rs (Phase 12): split at cached power B = base^(2^k), recurse on x div B and x mod B, combine as high * B + low.

  • Reduces base-10 conversion from O(n^2) repeated-divmod to O(M(n) log n); I9 roundtrip required.

DoubleLimb u128; holds one full Limb * Limb + carry_in product without arithmetic overflow.

  • Strictly a computation type; never stored in a BigUint limb array.
  • Used as the accumulator in schoolbook multiply's inner loop.

Extended GCD Euclidean algorithm extended to track Bezout pair (u, v).

  • bn-gcd/src/ext_gcd.rs; outputs (gcd, u, v) as (BigUint, BigInt, BigInt).
  • Basis for mod_inv in the composite-modulus case (Phase 11).

FFT (floating-point) Classic fast Fourier transform using complex f64 arithmetic; explicitly NOT used here (P5).

  • Rounding errors accumulate and can corrupt large products; replaced entirely by NTT.

In-place operation Kernel operating on borrowed LimbsMut<'a> slices, mutating output without heap allocation.

  • Mandatory for hot paths (bn-add, bn-mul, bn-div) to avoid per-iteration allocation (P6).
  • LimbsMut<'a> defined in bn-uint/src/view.rs.

Karatsuba multiplication O(n^1.585) divide-and-conquer multiply in bn-mul/src/karatsuba.rs (Phase 5).

  • Split: x = x1*B + x0; three sub-products z0=x0*y0, z2=x1*y1, z1=(x0+x1)*(y0+y1)-z0-z2.
  • Reconstruct: z0 + z1*B + z2*B^2; base case → schoolbook below KARATSUBA_THRESHOLD; I4 verified.

Knuth Algorithm D Multi-precision normalized long division (Phase 7, bn-div/src/knuth_d.rs; TAOCP Vol 2 §4.3.1).

  • Normalize divisor (left-shift so top bit is set); estimate quotient digit from top two dividend limbs.
  • Correct by at most 2 via compare-subtract; multiply-subtract; add-back on rare over-estimate.
  • Exactly satisfies I5 (a == q*b + r, 0 <= r < b).

Lehmer GCD Accelerated GCD for large inputs in bn-gcd/src/lehmer.rs (Phase 11).

  • Approximate both operands by their top one or two limbs; run multiple Euclidean steps in 64-bit arithmetic.
  • Accumulate results into a 2×2 matrix; apply once to full-precision values.
  • More efficient than binary GCD above ~4 limbs; reduces number of expensive multi-precision divisions.

Limb u64; the unit of magnitude storage; index 0 is the least-significant word (little-endian).

  • A BigUint is Vec<Limb>; index k stores bits [64k, 64k+64) of the magnitude.

Little-endian limb order Limb index 0 = bits 0..63 (least significant); index n-1 = most significant.

  • Matches natural carry-propagation loop direction (for i in 0..n); enables in-place growth by appending.

Magnitude Unsigned absolute value of a BigInt; stored as a BigUint.

  • Sign is a separate Sign field (P7); all limb-level arithmetic is sign-agnostic.

Modular inverse Integer x with a*x ≡ 1 (mod m), existing when gcd(a, m) == 1.

  • General case: extended GCD (bn-gcd/src/ext_gcd.rs).
  • Prime modulus: Fermat's little theorem (a^(p-2) mod p).
  • Returns None/error when gcd(a,m) != 1; result always in [1, m) (Phase 11).

Montgomery form Represent a mod m as aR mod m for R = 2^(64*k), the smallest limb-base power exceeding m.

  • Multiplication via REDC; no trial division in the inner loop.
  • Context Montgomery { modulus, n_prime, r2 } in bn-mod/src/montgomery.rs (Phase 10, I7).

MulAlgo Enum {Schoolbook, Karatsuba, Toom3, Ntt}; dispatch in bn-mul/src/lib.rs by limb-count vs. thresholds.

  • Observable via public accessor for testing; supports P4 and P10 (algorithm selection traceable).

Newton-reciprocal division Compute floor(R^k / d) by Newton-Raphson iteration from a single-word seed (bn-div/src/newton.rs, Phase 8).

  • Each iteration doubles the number of correct bits; convergence quadratic.
  • Quotient: q = (a * recip) >> shift plus a bounded correction; turns large division into multiplication; verified I6.

Normalization Strip all trailing zero limbs from a Vec<Limb> after any mutating operation (I1).

  • normalize() in bn-uint/src/normalize.rs; called by every mutating public entry point.
  • No-op on already-canonical values; idempotent (property-tested in Phase 1).

NTT (Number-Theoretic Transform) Discrete transform over Z_p for a Proth prime p = c*2^k + 1; exact integer arithmetic, no rounding.

  • Forward NTT, pointwise product, inverse NTT, CRT recombination yield the exact integer product (bn-fftmul, Phase 6, P5, I8).

NTT prime Prime p = c * 2^k + 1 ensuring existence of a primitive n-th root of unity in Z_p for n | (p-1).

  • Table in bn-fftmul/src/primes.rs; multiple primes used in parallel for CRT coverage.

NttPlan Struct {primes: &'static [u64], roots: Vec<u64>, len: usize} built by bn-fftmul/src/plan.rs.

  • Encapsulates prime selection and transform length for a given operand-size pair.
  • Immutable once constructed; shared between forward-transform and CRT-recombination passes.

Oracle The trusted reference implementation against which a faster algorithm is differentially verified.

  • Schoolbook multiplier (Phase 4) is the oracle for Karatsuba/Toom3/NTT (I4).
  • Knuth D is the oracle for Newton-reciprocal division (I6).
  • num-bigint crate and Python int are external oracles for Phase 14 conformance (P1, P10).

REDC Montgomery Reduction: given T < m*R, computes T * R^-1 mod m without expensive division.

  • Uses only adds, subtracts, and one multiply by n_prime = -m^-1 mod R; one conditional subtraction at the end.
  • All Montgomery-form multiplications in bn-mod use REDC exclusively.

Root of unity Primitive element w in Z_p with w^n = 1 and w^j ≠ 1 for 0 < j < n.

  • NTT twiddle factors are powers of w; computed or looked up per prime and per transform length.
  • Stored in NttPlan.roots.

Schoolbook multiplication O(n*m) naive double-loop multiply in bn-mul/src/schoolbook.rs (Phase 4).

  • Accumulate limb_of_a[i] * limb_of_b[j] into a u128 per-column with carry.
  • The unconditional correctness baseline; oracle for all faster algorithms (P1).

Sign Enum {Minus, NoSign, Plus} in bn-core/src/types.rs.

  • NoSign iff the associated magnitude is zero (I2); three-valued to make zero unambiguous.
  • Minus: strictly negative; Plus: strictly positive.

Sign-magnitude representation BigInt = {Sign, BigUint}; sign and magnitude stored separately (P7).

  • All limb kernels operate on magnitudes; sign rules isolated to bn-int/src/arith.rs.
  • Prevents sign confusion leaking into carry/borrow logic.

Sliding-window exponentiation Variant of modpow scanning exponent bits for contiguous non-zero windows of width w.

  • Precomputes table of odd powers up to 2^(w-1); roughly n/w + 2^(w-1) multiplications for n-bit exponent.
  • Implemented in bn-mod/src/modpow.rs (Phase 11); window width tuned by benchmarks.

Squaring Dedicated O(n^2/2) multiply path in bn-mul/src/schoolbook.rs (Phase 4).

  • Diagonal pairs a[i]*a[i] contribute once; off-diagonal a[i]*a[j] (i<j) accumulated and doubled.
  • Approximately 2× faster than general mul(a,a).

Toom-Cook-3 O(n^1.465) polynomial-evaluation multiplication in bn-mul/src/toom3.rs (Phase 5).

  • Split each operand into thirds; evaluate at 5 points {0, 1, -1, 2, ∞}; multiply pointwise (5 sub-multiplications).
  • Interpolate to recover 5 product polynomial coefficients by exact integer linear algebra.
  • All interpolation divisions are exact (no rounding); I4 verified.

Two's-complement bitwise semantics BigInt AND/OR/XOR/NOT behave as if operands have infinite sign-extension bits in two's complement.

  • Positive: infinite leading 0-bits; negative: infinite leading 1-bits.
  • Implemented by case analysis on Sign in bn-int/src/bitops.rs (Phase 9).
  • Behavior matches Python int and Java BigInteger; verified against Python reference.

Windowed modpow Fixed-width window modular exponentiation in bn-mod/src/modpow.rs (Phase 11).

  • Precompute table of 2^(w-1) odd powers of the base mod m.
  • Scan exponent MSB to LSB in w-bit groups; perform w squarings + 1 multiply per group.
  • Constant-time variant uses non-branching table lookup (P9).

fuzz/fuzz_targets/fuzz_roundtrip.rs Fuzz base-conversion roundtrip for bases 2..36 and arbitrary BigUint inputs; I9
benches/mul_bench.rs Criterion benchmarks: schoolbook / Karatsuba / Toom3 / NTT at log-spaced limb counts 1..10000
benches/div_bench.rs Criterion benchmarks: Knuth D vs. Newton-reciprocal at several divisor and dividend sizes
benches/modpow_bench.rs Criterion benchmarks: Barrett vs. Montgomery modpow at 512/1024/2048/4096-bit moduli
benches/convert_bench.rs Criterion benchmarks: base-10/16 conversion schoolbook vs. divide-and-conquer at 100/1000/10000-digit inputs
tests/conformance/mod.rs Integration test root; deterministic proptest seed; shared helpers; module imports
tests/conformance/vs_num_bigint.rs Differential tests comparing all operations against num-bigint crate; I4, I5, I6
tests/conformance/vs_python.rs Subprocess tests invoking Python 3 int for mul, divmod, pow, gcd, str; I4, I6, I8, I9
tests/size_regression.rs Assert no unexpected allocation increase at fixed benchmark operand sizes; refactor regression guard

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

All 14 phases plus a pre-coding setup sprint (Phase 0). Durations are calendar-week estimates for one focused engineer. Total: approximately 28.5 weeks. Exit criteria map to system invariants; key risks are phase-specific.

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
0 — Setup Workspace skeleton; CI pipeline (fmt/clippy/test/bench/fuzz); dev-dependency lock 0.5 wk Toolchain drift; cargo-fuzz nightly compatibility; CI cost CI green on empty stubs; fuzz targets compile; clippy --deny warnings clean
1 — Core types + magnitude bn-core types; BigUint little-endian; normalization; comparison; from/to u64/u128 1.5 wk Trailing-zero strip off-by-one; From<u128> byte order; Ord inconsistency Property tests: normalize idempotent; total order; from/to roundtrip; I1 and I2 hold
2 — Addition + subtraction bn-add carry/borrow; add_assign/sub_assign; add_mul_1 primitive 1.5 wk Carry dropped on final limb; sub(a<b) silent wrong answer a+b-b==a; a-a==0; 24h fuzz clean; sub(a<b) panics/errors deterministically; I3
3 — Shifts + bitwise + bit utils bn-uint shl/shr; bit_len; count_ones; trailing_zeros; set_bit; test_bit 1 wk Cross-limb shift off-by-one; normalization lost after right-shift Shift round-trip; bit_len == ceil(log2(x+1)); normalization preserved
4 — Schoolbook mul + squaring bn-mul O(n*m) schoolbook; dedicated squaring; result length n+m 1.5 wk u128 overflow audit; squaring off-diagonal double-count mul(a,b)==mul(b,a); sqr(a)==mul(a,a); length exactly n+m; differential vs. Python for 10^5 pairs
5 — Karatsuba + Toom-Cook bn-mul Karatsuba; Toom-Cook-3; threshold dispatch 2 wk Karatsuba middle-term sign error; Toom3 interpolation non-exact; threshold regression I4: Karatsuba == Toom3 == schoolbook for 10^5 inputs at and across both thresholds
6 — NTT multiplication bn-fftmul NTT; Proth prime table; CRT recombination; NttPlan builder 3 wk CRT overflow if product exceeds prime product; wrong root of unity; integer overflow in butterfly I8: NTT == schoolbook exact for up to 10^6 limbs; 24h fuzz clean; no floating-point anywhere
7 — Knuth Algorithm D bn-div normalized long division; ≤2 digit correction; add-back; single-limb fast path 2 wk Digit over-estimate > 2 (must verify impossibility); normalization shift asymmetry I5: a==q*b+r, 0<=r<b for 10^6 pairs; div-by-zero → typed error; no UB
8 — Newton-reciprocal division bn-div Newton reciprocal; quotient from reciprocal + correction; reuse bn-mul 2 wk Convergence failure for near-power-of-2 divisors; off-by-1 correction; quadratic convergence absent I6: Newton == Knuth for all random inputs; large divrem demonstrably uses multiply path (bench)
9 — Signed BigInt bn-int sign dispatch; sign-rule arith; two's-complement bitwise; From<i64/i128> 1.5 wk Zero result left as Plus/Minus (violates I2); NOT for Minus wrong magnitude I2: a+(-a)==0; (-a)*b==-(a*b); bitwise matches Python reference for ±2^k±1; zero always NoSign
10 — Barrett + Montgomery bn-mod Barrett precompute + reduce; Montgomery REDC; mod_mul/mod_add/mod_sub 2 wk mu off by 1 at limb boundary; REDC roundtrip failure; result > m I7: Barrett == naive mod; REDC roundtrip holds; all results in [0,m) under 10^6 fuzz inputs
11 — Modpow + GCD + inverse bn-mod windowed/constant-time modpow; bn-gcd binary/Lehmer/ext-gcd; modular inverse 2 wk Constant-time leak via branch or cache-line pattern; Lehmer 2×2 matrix overflow in 64-bit `gcd(a,b)
12 — Base conversion + parse bn-convert schoolbook + divide-and-conquer; validated ASCII parse; ParseError 1.5 wk DC split power off-by-1; invalid-char panic instead of ParseError I9: roundtrip bases 2..36 for 10,000-limb inputs; fuzz parse never panics; ParseError returned
13 — Rationals + public API bn-rational construct/reduce/arithmetic; finalize ops::*, From/TryFrom, Display 2 wk GCD-reduce misses a factor; rational division-by-zero undetected; stable-Rust incompatibility I10 canonicality; a/b+c/d matches reference; API compiles on stable Rust; no undocumented unsafe
14 — Hardening + conformance + fuzz + perf Differential tests vs. num-bigint/Python; 24h fuzz corpus; benchmarks; cargo publish --dry-run 2 wk Python subprocess CI flakiness; fuzz corpus misses large sizes; benchmark regression All conformance tests green; 24h fuzz clean; mul/div within 2× num-bigint; docs complete; publish passes
Total Full library: correct, tested, fuzz-clean, benchmarked, publishable ~28.5 wk Scope creep; late threshold re-tuning; CRT prime coverage gap All 14 phase exit criteria met; CI green; no open invariant violations; README accurate
